href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1487s\\\">24:47</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>Okay, so the summary so far is without watermarking. We often sample without a random seed because I know most people don\u2019t even use one. I honestly don\u2019t think you can necessarily do it with the Claude and OpenAI APIs. I know you can do it in Ollama, but I also always had some problems with that because I used Ollama in one of my books for the bonus material to generate some texts.</p><p>I was fixing the random seed, but it still wasn\u2019t always deterministic, and so forth. So it\u2019s tricky. Your mileage may also vary, depending on the software version and so forth. Anyways, so without watermarking, we have this random sampling. With watermarking on the right-hand side, we still have the random sampling. But in addition to just a random sampling being fully random, we have this watermarking key.</p><p>And this watermarking key is passed to the random seed generator to set a specific random seed, making this deterministic. But it\u2019s essentially very similar, and like I mentioned, there\u2019s a lot of benefit in terms of understanding things from scratch. And now we know essentially where this watermark is applied to. So this is essentially applied to the sampling. It\u2019s not applied inside the LLM, which is actually cool knowledge.</p><p>So they don\u2019t need to train a new LLM for that. They can just use an existing LLM, and they just apply it at this sampling stage. They don\u2019t have to retrain anything or anything like that. So yeah, that is actually interesting, right?</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1573s\\\">26:13</a></p><h2>How Watermark Detection Works</h2><div href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1581s\\\">26:21</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>But we are not quite done yet. I would also like to talk about how we can understand or see whether text is watermarked. So detecting the watermark is only possible if we have access to the key. So, for example, if we have these different texts, and essentially, after the text was generated, you find some random text on the internet (for example, you find this text number four here on the internet somewhere), you want to know: is this watermarked?</p><p>Well, it\u2019s impossible to know because, in order to know, you would need the watermarking key. You need this scoring function, and then you have to score basically the text with a scoring function. And then the idea is that if the score is above a certain threshold, then the text is watermarked. Otherwise, it\u2019s not watermarked. But as the end user, we can\u2019t do this because we don\u2019t have this key. So the key is not available to us.</p><p>Only Anthropic will have the key. However, in this blog post, they mentioned that they are providing it, of course, or they\u2019re going to develop an API for that that they will make available. I don\u2019t know. Honestly, I\u2019m not affiliated. I don\u2019t know the details. I was just reading this in this blog post. That\u2019s all I know. So that API might as well be private for some companies, like, let\u2019s say, X or Substack Notes, when they want to label AI-generated posts.</p><p>They may make it public for end users to use. Who knows? We will have to wait on that. But yeah, so the bottom line here is that watermark detection is only possible if we have this watermarking key or, of course, the API that they are going to develop.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1680s\\\">28:00</a></p><h2>How to Remove a Watermark</h2><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 So now that we know how the watermarking works, we also know the shortcomings. I mean, this is really highly dependent on specific tokens in certain positions. So, for example, in this given text, if these colored words or tokens are the watermarking positions, we know that we could remove the watermark by editing this, right? If we change all the words at these positions, we would be 100% able to defeat this watermark.</p><p>Now, the problem, though, is that we don\u2019t know, right?</p><div><hr></div><div href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1721s\\\">28:41</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>So we don\u2019t know where these words are because we haven\u2019t generated the watermark. So we don\u2019t know which positions to look at. So the practical scenario here is that we could just randomly edit the text. So we would randomly change a few words and hope that we change enough positions to edit the watermark. So that would be one way to remove it. And since we also don\u2019t know which are the highest-scoring ones, because that would require us to have access to the LLM and rerun the prompt through the LLM to find out which words are the highest-scoring, we can kind of only guess.</p><p>So for example, we might say, oh, we replace \u201Covercast\u201D with \u201Ccloudy\u201D because we don\u2019t know that \u201Cgray\u201D was high-scoring, you know? So in this case, it might be intuitive to say \u201Cgray\u201D, but there might be cases where it\u2019s not so intuitive. So what I\u2019m trying to illustrate here is just some general text editing where we are modifying positions, but we are still kind of guessing what a watermark position is.</p><p>So since we don\u2019t know, we added just a few words here and there. And if we added enough words, that would also defeat the watermark.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1817s\\\">30:17</a></p><h2>How the Watermark href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1799s\\\">29:59</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>So yeah, that was the watermarking in a nutshell. I mentioned that there is a scoring function to find out whether something is watermarked. And I want to do it as a bonus here. It\u2019s already a long video, but as a bonus here, I wanted to briefly also explain how this scoring function works because that is also interesting information. It\u2019s a bit complicated. It\u2019s not essential to understand how the scoring function works.</p><p>But the reason why they do it the certain way they do is to make the detection cheaper. Because otherwise, if I go back one slide or two slides, if you wanted to check if something is watermarked, if even they wanted to check, they would have to rerun the prompt to get these scores and then apply this watermarking random seed to get this text and then compare. And that would be very expensive because then essentially every text you want to compare, you would have to rerun the LLM.</p><p>You have to know which LLM, and that would be really unfeasible because you often also don\u2019t even know the prompt, right? So yeah, so they have like a trick that they use to, yeah, I would say, modify the sampling so that you don\u2019t use or don\u2019t need the LLM later on for the scoring stage. And in the blog post, they mentioned that they derived this method from a paper.</p><p>It was a Nature paper, and this method is called SynthID-Text. So that was like a paper that came out maybe one or two years ago. It was by Google, and they use a similar technique they call Claude watermarking. I don\u2019t know, sorry, I don\u2019t know if they use exactly that technique, but that\u2019s the one they mentioned.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1878s\\\">31:18</a></p><h2>SynthID Text and Tournament Sampling</h2><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a So before we looked at the slides, we looked at the regular, let\u2019s say, overview here, where we have some text. We put it through the LLM. We get this logit distribution and then we sample from the distribution and get the output token. And here, during the sampling, we use the watermarking key and the random seed generator. So this is still correct. This is still what\u2019s going on, but there is a bit more, I guess, nuance to how this token is sampled.</p><p>So they\u2019re not just using, let\u2019s say, NumPy\u2019s random choice. They\u2019re using something a bit more sophisticated here.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 is-viewable-img\\\" href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=1936s\\\">32:16</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>So assume, again, our context is \u201Cthe weather today is cold,\u201D and we want to generate the next token. So, for example: \u201Cgray\u201D, \u201Covercast\u201D, \u201Cgloomy\u201D, \u201Ccloudy\u201D. \u201CGray\u201D is 50% probability, \u201Covercast\u201D is 30, \u201Cgloomy\u201D is 15. Let\u2019s say \u201Ccloudy\u201D is 0.05 and the rest is, let\u2019s say, 0. Here it looks, of course, a bit different. Let\u2019s say that\u2019s \u201Cgray\u201D and \u201Covercast\u201D. I\u2019m just reusing this figure. But now imagine these are the most likely ones, like \u201Cgray\u201D and \u201Covercast\u201D, and everything else is just very small, except \u201Cgloomy\u201D and \u201Ccloudy,\u201D maybe.</p><p>So essentially, think about just a very small vocabulary for this example of four words instead of all these 50 words here, just to make it even simpler. Now, as I mentioned before, we could use \u2018sNumPy\u2019s random choice with these probabilities to sample the next token.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2015s\\\">33:35</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>So instead, what they use, they also use it during the sampling, during the generation, so that it can be reused later during detection. What they use is called tournament sampling. So this is instead of using something like random choice, they use a concept called tournament sampling. And so how does that work? It might look a bit complicated, but it looks really more complicated than it really is, to be honest.</p><p>So you might have to, I guess, stop the video at some point and just sit with the figure a bit. But I think it is actually simpler than it looks like. It\u2019s like once you get the hang of it, it\u2019s pretty straightforward. But let me try to explain here. So what we have is we have still this context, and then we have these probable or plausible next tokens with these different probabilities.</p><p>Now they have something they call random watermarking functions.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2073s\\\">34:33</a></p><h2>Random Watermarking Functions</h2><div href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2075s\\\">34:35</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>Here we have three watermarking functions, G1, G2, and G3. In reality, they might have 30, 50, or even more. Here I\u2019m just using three because that is simpler on this slide. It\u2019s just smaller, you know, like it fits better on the slide. Now, if we look at this word \u201Cgray\u201D, this might give us a signature 101. With that, I mean, if we use this watermarking key to generate this random seed, and we have three functions, G1, G2, G3.</p><p>If I put the word \u201Cgray\u201D, what I\u2019m skipping here is that usually you put the word \u201Cgray\u201D together with the four or three previous words from the context. So it\u2019s \u201Ccold\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D. If I put that into G1 together with this watermarking key, I get the value one. Why? Well, that\u2019s just how this function works. It\u2019s like a random function. The random function either returns zero or one. In this case, with this random key and this token, it returns one.</p><p>With the same key, but a different function, you get the value zero. And then here you get a one again. So if we have more functions (of course, 30 functions), this will be a very long string of ones and zeros.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a American football, you always have two teams playing against each other. And that\u2019s kind of like the same idea. We have a pair of tokens, and they\u2019re playing against each other, essentially. And the scores, they come from these functions here. So we start with the first function in the first round. So we have \u201Ccloudy\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D.</p><p>So we look up here: \u201Cgray\u201D is a one and \u201Ccloudy\u201D is a one. Okay. So one and one. \u201COvercast\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D. So \u201Covercast\u201D is zero, \u201Cgray\u201D is one. So we have zero, one. \u201CGloomy\u201D and \u201Covercast\u201D. So here we have \u201Cgloomy\u201D zero, \u201Covercast\u201D zero. So zero, zero. And then we have \u201Cgray\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D again, because we are running out. So we don\u2019t have enough of the others. So we have one duplicate.</p><p>So this is chosen randomly. And so you have one and one here. Now we look at the results. So this is a tie. In the case of a tie, we also select randomly using, you know, the random seed and the watermarking key. So here, \u201Ccloudy\u201D survives. And from this one, G1 is, according to G1, \u201Cgray\u201D is the winner because it has the one. So \u201Cgray\u201D survives. And then here, \u201Covercast\u201D and \u201C gray \u201C are a tie, randomly selected, and \u201Cgray\u201D also randomly selected.</p><p>So we have now \u201Ccloudy\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D and \u201Covercast\u201D and \u201Cgray\u201D. And we play the next round in this tournament. So in this next round, we use G2. So according to G2, \u201Ccloudy\u201D has a zero here. \u201CGray\u201D also has zero. \u201COvercast\u201D has one. And \u201Cgray\u201D also has zero, sorry. And so, the next stage of the tournament again.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 is-viewable-img\\\" target=\\\"_blank\\\" And then we look again at the scores. So \u201Cgray\u201D has a one. \u201COvercast\u201D is a zero. So \u201Cgray\u201D is the winner. And that\u2019s how the token \u201Cgray\u201D is sampled. What is the watermarking key doing here? So the watermarking key, if I go back a few slides, is selected for generating these scores using these random watermarking functions.</p><p>So the watermarking key determines essentially what values we get at these stages. So the watermarking key is still very important. Otherwise, these signatures would look different.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2358s\\\">39:18</a></p><h2>Detecting Watermarks Without Rerunning And that\u2019s just how this modified sampling procedure works. We could have used NumPy\u2019s `random.choice`. But the shortcoming of that is that if we want to score random text on the internet, we would have to rerun the LLM. With this technique, we don\u2019t. I will show you in a moment. So this technique sounds like really weird and cumbersome, but it has the advantage that we can now score random text more easily without having to rerun the LLM.</p><p>So it\u2019s essentially just to make the detection easier and cheaper.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 is-viewable-img\\\" target=\\\"_blank\\\" href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2388s\\\">39:48</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>So, for example, if we have a new text. So I\u2019m just using the same text here, but let\u2019s assume it\u2019s new text. So this is after the sampling, when we are scoring. And let\u2019s say we are discovering this text on the internet. And the text is the weather today is cold and \u201Cgray\u201D, and we want to know if this is LLM-generated or not. So we would, or Claude/Anthropic would, have the watermarking key and these functions: G1, G2, and G3.</p><p>And it would put this text through these functions. For the one position here for \u201Cgray\u201D, we would get 101, similar to what we got during the generation process. So this is the same as before. And this has, if we add up these bits, two bits, right? One and one here. So it has two bits of information, let\u2019s say, for simplicity. This is just a really simple illustration.</p><p>But let\u2019s assume we get a score of two here for the \u201Cgray\u201D in this position. If we had a different word here, \u201Covercast,\u201D in this position, we would get one if we get \u201Cgloomy,\u201D like we also have one, and \u201Ccloudy\u201D one. So I\u2019m just summing over each row here, right? So that\u2019s just like a score we would get at each position. And here I\u2019m only looking at the last position.</p><p>If I would do this at other positions, I would get a different score at different positions. So, for example, let\u2019s assume at the first position I get a two. Here I get a two. For \u201Ctoday\u201D, I get a three. For \u201Cis\u201D, I get a two. \u201CCold\u201D, two. And \u201Cgray\u201D, three. So here I\u2019m applying these watermarking functions as I\u2019ve shown on the previous slide.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 All I need is the watermarking key, the random seed generator, and these functions, G1, G2, and G3. And that\u2019s all I need.</p><p>I don\u2019t need the LLM. And I can get this score here. But for example, we can say if the score is greater than two, then the text is watermarked. If the score is smaller than two, it\u2019s not watermarked. So here, if the score is greater than two, it\u2019s a yes. So yes, this is watermarked. In this case, 1.71 is not greater than two. So this text is not watermarked. Okay. So that\u2019s just the way we can then detect whether random text on the internet is watermarked or not.</p><p>It\u2019s essentially just applying these watermarking functions and then averaging over the scores and applying a threshold. We could also use something like NumPy\u2019s random choice with a random seed or to make the sampling deterministic. But then again, it would be hard to score any text on the internet.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2606s\\\">43:26</a></p><h2>Watermarking Summary The thing that is different between no watermarking and watermarking is that we are controlling this sampling here with the watermarking key. And inside that, we have this tournament sampling. And yeah, as I mentioned before, detecting the watermarks requires the secret key and the watermarking functions G1 to Gn.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2634s\\\">43:54</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>And again, removing the watermark, because I think that\u2019s maybe interesting to some people, would ideally involve editing all the positions here. But since we don\u2019t know which positions are watermarked and internally, they choose the positions so that they have equally likely tokens at those positions. And there might be positions where that\u2019s not true. So here, for example, for \u201Ctrees\u201D, we might not even have an alternative word that is high scoring so they don\u2019t watermark that position.</p><p>So they only do the watermarking at certain positions essentially. Since we don\u2019t know which positions to kind of defeat or remove the watermark, we would...</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 So I think if there\u2019s a person who likes to use AI-generated text everywhere on the internet, let\u2019s say there\u2019s a news website that likes to use AI-generated text to write the news, I don\u2019t think watermarking will necessarily stop them from doing that. They will probably still want to generate AI-generated text because that\u2019s part of their workflow. So I think my guess is that they\u2019ll use another model.</p><div><hr></div><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 is-viewable-img\\\" href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2708s\\\">45:08</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>They\u2019ll just use a second model to edit the text to get the so-called edited AI-generated text. So it\u2019s complicating the pipeline. Instead of getting the text directly from Claude, it\u2019s now using Claude to generate AI-generated text, passing it through a local model, and then having edited AI-generated text, which is likely not watermarked anymore. So why a local model? I just think a local model because I think all the providers- the proprietary LLMs, not only Claude, but also Google\u2014 I mean, Google wrote this paper, right?</p><p>So I\u2019m thinking that they are also watermarking Gemini text. And I think OpenAI is probably already doing it or will do so as well. I mean, I\u2019m just speculating, but I\u2019m imagining everyone will probably do something like that because there\u2019s like an EU regulation that requires that. And that\u2019s, according to the blog post, apparently why Claude is doing it. Yeah, so I\u2019m thinking local models may not, at least not yet, implement this watermarking.</p><p>So I think people will just use a local model and then generate edited AI-generated text. And my guess is it will be slightly worse than the original text because for the local model, you might now be using a smaller model. So, I mean, you could also technically just use the local model directly to generate text. But in my view, editing text is simpler than generating text. So for the generation of the text, you might use a very expensive high-end, I don\u2019t know, like the highest, most expensive Claude model for complicated text.</p><p>And then you use a cheaper local model to make these surgical edits, essentially. That\u2019s probably what\u2019s going to happen. And why worse? So if we look back at this graphic where we just added random positions, you might be just changing words for the sake of changing them. And then it risks making the text worse. So you might still have generated text, but it\u2019s kind of like it\u2019s edited awkwardly.</p><div><hr></div><p><a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2833s\\\">47:13</a></p><h2>Final Thoughts</h2><div class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\"><figure><a class=\\\"image-link image2 href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tLv7qRWFMlw&amp;t=2840s\\\">47:20</a></figcaption></figure></div><p>But anyway, so my goal here was to explain how the watermarking works and not, let\u2019s say, the worldwide ramifications of that. But I hope this kind of behind-the-scenes, under-the-hood look is useful. The watermarking is not as complicated as it might seem, but I think it was still 52 slides, so it was also not super trivial. So I hope you found this little lecture useful. And yeah, until next time, see you then.</p><p></p><div><hr></div><p>PS: If you like more explainers in this style, I don\u2019t post videos to YouTube regularly, but I have accumulated over 300 videos over the years, which you can find on <a href=\\\"https://www.youtube.com/@SebastianRaschka\\\"><span>my YouTube channel here</span></a>.</p><p></p>\",\"has_dynamic_content\":false,\"truncated_body_text\":\"I recently posted a Substack note about Claude\u2019s new watermarking process and implementation. Since it\u2019s such a popular topic and sparked such a lively discussion, I thought it might be interesting to go into a bit more detail when explaining how it works.\",\"wordcount\":7771,\"post_preview_limit\":1000,\"language\":\"en\",\"postTags\":[{\"id\":\"5e263784-abbd-4549-a8c3-69a981a6c7f2\",\"publication_id\":1174659,\"name\":\"AI\",\"slug\":\"ai\",\"hidden\":false},{\"id\":\"9e17a4ee-db57-4ead-a40d-e6a0628c8331\",\"publication_id\":1174659,\"name\":\"GPT\",\"slug\":\"gpt\",\"hidden\":false},{\"id\":\"d363cca5-e117-4aed-8ef4-0c07863fea32\",\"publication_id\":1174659,\"name\":\"LLMs\",\"slug\":\"llms\",\"hidden\":false},{\"id\":\"e578c3cb-819a-4abf-937e-e95dc32cbb48\",\"publication_id\":1174659,\"name\":\"Claude\",\"slug\":\"claude\",\"hidden\":false}],\"postCountryBlocks\":[],\"headlineTest\":null,\"coverImagePalette\":{\"Vibrant\":{\"rgb\":[28,212,213],\"population\":142},\"DarkVibrant\":{\"rgb\":[32,113,112],\"population\":79},\"LightVibrant\":{\"rgb\":[137.8058091286307,239.04398340248963,239.5941908713693],\"population\":0},\"Muted\":{\"rgb\":[148,148,132],\"population\":1},\"DarkMuted\":{\"rgb\":[92,76,44],\"population\":1},\"LightMuted\":{\"rgb\":[190,190,187],\"population\":10}},\"publishedBylines\":[{\"id\":27393275,\"name\":\"Sebastian Raschka, PhD\",\"handle\":\"rasbt\",\"previous_name\":\"Sebastian Raschka\",\"photo_url\":\"https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fbucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F61f4c017-506f-4e9b-a24f-76340dad0309_800x800.jpeg\",\"bio\":\"I'm an LLM research engineer 10+ years of experience in artificial intelligence. The walkthrough of token sampling and watermark detection makes a tricky topic really clear.\"}]}]},\"publication_id\":1174659,\"post_id\":211923328,\"user_id\":528467631,\"ancestor_path\":\"\",\"type\":\"comment\",\"deleted\":false,\"date\":\"2026-08-22T18:26:25.730Z\",\"edited_at\":null,\"status\":\"published\",\"pinned_by_user_id\":null,\"restacks\":0,\"name\":\"Felipe Mart\u00EDnez Cutillas\",\"photo_url\":\"https://substack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/e224521f-69ee-4212-8bcf-dae90d8ba967_512x512.png\",\"handle\":\"felipemartinezcutillas\",\"reactor_names\":[\"Sebastian Raschka, PhD\"],\"reaction\":null,\"reactions\":{\"\u2764\":2},\"reaction_count\":2,\"children\":[],\"bans\":[],\"suppressed\":false,\"user_banned\":false,\"user_banned_for_comment\":false,\"user_slug\":\"felipemartinezcutillas\",\"metadata\":{\"is_author\":false,\"membership_state\":null,\"eligibleForGift\":true,\"author_on_other_pub\":{\"name\":\"Felipe Mart\u00EDnez Cutillas\",\"id\":9993325,\"base_url\":\"https://felipemcutillas.substack.com\"}},\"user_bestseller_tier\":null,\"can_dm\":true,\"userStatus\":{\"bestsellerTier\":null,\"subscriberTier\":null,\"leaderboard\":null,\"vip\":false,\"badge\":null,\"subscriber\":null},\"score\":7,\"children_count\":1,\"reported_by_user\":false,\"restacked\":false,\"childrenSummary\":\"1 reply by Sebastian Raschka, PhD\"},{\"id\":320334079,\"body\":\"What I found most valuable here is that you didn't frame watermarking as simply \u201Cgood\u201D or \u201Cbad\u201D\u2014you actually explained the mechanism well enough for readers to form their own opinion.\\n\\nThe distinction between watermarking at the 'sampling stage' rather than retraining the model was particularly interesting. It also makes the tournament-sampling approach easier to appreciate: detection can happen later without having to rerun the entire LLM.\\n\\nBut the broader implication may be even more important. If watermarking becomes widespread, attempts to remove it could lead people to pass AI-generated text through other models, potentially creating a new problem where the pursuit of \u201Cundetectable\u201D text actually makes the output worse.\\n\\nI really appreciate explainers like this that go beyond announcing a new feature and help readers understand 'what is actually happening underneath the hood'.\",\"body_json\":{\"type\":\"doc\",\"attrs\":{\"schemaVersion\":\"v1\",\"title\":null},\"content\":[{\"type\":\"paragraph\",\"content\":[{\"type\":\"text\",\"text\":\"What I found most valuable here is that you didn't frame watermarking as simply \u201Cgood\u201D or \u201Cbad\u201D\u2014you actually explained the mechanism well enough for readers to form their own opinion.\"}]},{\"type\":\"paragraph\",\"content\":[{\"type\":\"text\",\"text\":\"The distinction between watermarking at the 'sampling stage' rather than retraining the model was particularly interesting. It also makes the tournament-sampling approach easier to appreciate: detection can happen later without having to rerun the entire LLM.\"}]},{\"type\":\"paragraph\",\"content\":[{\"type\":\"text\",\"text\":\"But the broader implication may be even more important.
